**Technical Vocabulary**

<term>Binding</term>: The structural covering of a book, here made of leather.
<term>Leather</term>: Prepared animal skin used for durability and decoration.
<term>Spine</term>: The edge of the book where the pages are gathered and bound.
<term>Gold-stamped</term>: A method of decorating the binding by pressing gold leaf into the surface with a heated tool.
<term>Raised bands</term>: Horizontal ridges on the spine that hide the cords used to sew the book's sections together.

<summary>This page describes the exterior of the manuscript, noting a dark brown leather binding with gold-stamped lettering on the spine. The title is largely lost to time, leaving only fragmentary letters.</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, leather, spine, gold-stamping, raised bands, conservation, fragments</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1708 Amsterdam edition. It contains the "Avertissement" (Publisher’s Advertisement or Preface), which serves to authenticate the manuscript following the author's untimely death.</meta>

# ADVERTISEMENT

The Public has been so favorably disposed toward the *Count of Gabalis* <note>The original work, published in 1670, was a popular satire that used the concept of "elemental spirits" (sylphs, undines, etc.) to poke fun at both occultists and rationalist philosophers.</note>, that it was believed nothing more pleasing could be offered than what remained of the Author's manuscripts on the same subject. It is well known that Monsieur de Villars <note>Henri de Montfaucon de Villars (1635–1673). He was a cleric whose satirical writing made him many enemies.</note> was murdered on the road to Lyon, and that his papers were not found upon him. It has since been learned that they were in the hands of one of his relatives, who did not share them until very late.

As there is no one who has not read the first *Conversations of the Count of Gabalis* with pleasure, we believed we were giving a gift to the Public by providing these, which are no less curious than the first. In them, one will see a refined critique of the Philosophy of Monsieur Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650). His "New Philosophy" of rationalism was often the target of Villars' wit, as Villars contrasted it with the mysterious "Secret Sciences."</note>, and several very remarkable things concerning the New Philosophy.

<summary>This preface explains the origin of these posthumous papers, claiming they were recovered years after the author's mysterious murder to satisfy public demand for more "Conversations" on the occult.</summary>
<keywords>posthumous preface, Montfaucon de Villars, murder of the author, Cartesianism, New Philosophy, manuscript discovery</keywords>

### FIRST CONVERSATION.

I am * predestined to see an extraordinary man every year. Blessed be the Star <note>original: "l'Etoile"; a reference to the astrological belief that one’s life is governed by the positions of the stars at birth</note> that has given me, this year, Doctor Jean le Brun; and blessed be whichever of my friends or my enemies, who, to amuse themselves or to insult me, sent this excellent man to me.

* <note>This work appears thirty years after the death of its author.</note>

<vocab>Jean le Brun, secret sciences <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, these included alchemy, kabbalah, and magic</note>, predestined, Star, Conversations</vocab>

<summary>This opening page introduces a series of dialogues regarding esoteric and occult knowledge, framed as a chance meeting with a mysterious figure named Jean le Brun. The author attributes this meeting to astrological destiny and notes that the text is being published posthumously.</summary>
<keywords>Jean le Brun, secret sciences, esotericism, astrology, destiny, posthumous publication, occultism</keywords>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>New Conversations</header>

Anyone other than myself would perhaps have been offended by a certain compliment he first paid me; but I never make those kinds of mistakes. I find it beneficial to accommodate people who are unique in their kind; an eccentric <note>original: "un original"; a term for someone with a singular or odd character</note> is always of great value to me; and God has granted me the grace to recognize that fools <note>original: "fols"</note> are only in this world to provide lessons in wisdom. It is true that I had some need of that grace to avoid throwing Monsieur Jean le Brun out the door the first time he appeared to me. He knocked abruptly at my study <term>Cabinet: a private room used for reading, writing, and receiving important visitors</term>, and entered in the same manner, holding a book and a staff in one hand, and removing his large hat with the other. "Sir," he said to me, "I am your servant: I have come all the way from Ireland specifically to tell you that you are ill-advised." He had red and wild eyes, a pale face, a short black suit, a wool belt over his small cassock <note>original: "soutanelle"; a waist-length or knee-length clerical garment</note>, a peculiar beard, and the look and temperament <note>original: "le poil"; literally "the hair," used here to describe his rough or prickly outward character</note> of an offended zealot. "Sir,"

### New Conversations

<meta>catchword: is not</meta>

...councils <note>Original: "ciles", the conclusion of "conciles".</note> that formerly forbade reading you <note>The speaker is likely addressing Aristotle or his works directly.</note> were truly inspired by Heaven! Are you not of this opinion, Sir? Is *Aristotle* not pernicious to Morality? Pernicious, I replied, Sir, in the highest degree. And do you not find the century, he continued, horribly corrupt? Horribly, I replied. And would you not willingly become, he pursued, the Disciple and the Coadjutor <term>Coadjutor: an assistant or helper to a high-ranking official, often intended as a successor</term> of a man extraordinarily raised up by the Holy Spirit for the general reformation of morals? Provided that it were neither *Luther* nor *Calvin*, I replied, nor some other man of the same kind, I have a sufficient penchant for correcting. That, he said, is the most laudable inclination that a Christian can have: one must think only of morals; we are all Pastors <term>Pastors: used here in the sense of spiritual caretakers or "shepherds" of one another</term> to one another; God has charged each of us in particular with the salvation of our neighbor. Woe to him who works only for his own sanctification <term>Sanctification: the process of being made holy or purified from sin</term>, and who neglects that of his brothers: but this is not

### on the Secret Sciences.

...and who spend their lives meditating on new Laws, a new **Politics**, a new **Theology**, a new **Morality**, a new **Philosophy**, always possess some good and some ridiculous qualities. They have certain <term>lucid intervals<note>Periods of sanity or clarity experienced by those otherwise considered mentally unstable.</note></term>, in which there is something to be gained; one laughs at the rest and wonders at just how far the imagination of a **Man of Letters** can be exalted.

Sir, I said to *Joannes Brunus* <note>Latin for Giordano Bruno, a famous Renaissance philosopher and occultist.</note>, you shall not leave, if you please; you are tired from your long journey, you shall rest here. Here is a small day-bed where you may lie down for some time; and for the consultation you wish to make with the Lord, here is a <term>prie-dieu<note>A piece of furniture intended for private prayer, consisting of a kneeling rail and a small shelf for a prayer book.</note></term>. I, meanwhile, shall go get dressed; we will confer afterwards on your holy projects, then we shall dine, if it pleases you.

Ah! Sir, he said to me while embracing me, there is nothing so noble as you; I hope that God will inspire me to admit you to the <term>Apostolate<note>A mission or group dedicated to a specific religious or moral cause; here, Brunus’s self-styled group of reformers.</note></term> to which He has called me; go get dres—

## SECOND CONVERSATION.

Joannes Brunus <note>Latin for Giordano Bruno, the Italian friar and philosopher known for his theories on the infinite universe.</note> spent an hour in conference with the Holy Spirit: he emerged from my Study glowing like a Cherub <note>In traditional theology, Cherubim are angels associated with divine light and the presence of God.</note>. "You are one of us, my son," he said to me, "God has told me that the zeal you have for the reformation of morals comes from Him; that it is He who inspired your contempt for Aristotle <note>The narrator and Brunus share a rejection of Scholasticism, the medieval school of thought based on Aristotle’s logic.</note>; and that it is He who made you understand that the melancholic Descartes <note>René Descartes, the famous French philosopher; "melancholic" here likely refers to his solitary and analytical temperament.</note> does not deserve all the esteem that the Author of this Book would have one have for him. Upon these three foundations, I shall have no difficulty in telling you my designs, recounting my history, explaining my Philosophy, and associating you with the glory of reforming the Christian World. Sit—"

<meta>catchword: —down</meta>

<vocab>Joannes Brunus, Holy Spirit, Aristotle, Descartes, reformation, Philosophy, Christian World</vocab>

<summary>After an hour of private prayer, Joannes Brunus declares that the narrator has been divinely chosen as an ally because of his shared disdain for traditional philosophy, promising to reveal his grand plan for world reform.</summary>
<keywords>Joannes Brunus, Holy Spirit, Aristotle, René Descartes, reformation, philosophy, Christian World, divine inspiration</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Joannes Brunus’s explanation of why previous attempts to reform the Church from within have failed. He specifically critiques three types of reformers: those focused on ancient discipline, those focused on replacing Scholastic theology with Patristics (writings of the Church Fathers), and those who used satire to attack moral laxity.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>New Conversations</header>

if they could not succeed. One of them wished to first undertake the restoration of the ancient vigor of **Discipline** <note>In this religious context, *Discipline* refers to the internal regulations and moral conduct governing the clergy and the faithful.</note>, and the severity of the old **Canons** <note>original: *Canons*; these are the decrees and laws established by Church councils to govern ecclesiastical life.</note>. His design failed: one should not have gone so openly against the torrent of the corruption of the age; the human heart requires more careful handling. Another, in league with the first, made a prodigious study to change the face of all **Theology**, to discredit the **Scholastic Doctors** <note>The "Scholastics" were medieval theologians, like Thomas Aquinas, who used logic and philosophy to explain Christian doctrine. By the 17th and 18th centuries, many reformers saw their methods as overly dry and abstract.</note>, and to substitute reasoning with a science based on memory and collections of the **Church Fathers** <note>The influential early Christian teachers and writers, such as St. Augustine or St. Jerome, whose works were seen as more "pure" than medieval logic.</note>. This design was great and good: but, good God, what an undertaking! To directly challenge the **Pedants**, the **Universities**, and the **Monks**! May God have mercy on his soul—I told him one day that his project lacked prudence, and that he would provoke too many people to take up arms against him. Another made a great stir with his mockeries regarding certain alleged relaxations <note>This likely refers to the "laxism" in moral theology, which critics argued made it too easy for people to sin and still receive absolution.</note>: but besides the fact that few people believed he was acting in good faith in his citations, many...

<meta>catchword: blow</meta>

<summary>Joannes Brunus critiques three specific failed reform strategies: the return to ancient legal rigor, the attempt to replace logical Scholasticism with early Church writings, and the use of satire to expose moral laxity.</summary>
<keywords>Ecclesiastical Discipline, Canon Law, Scholasticism, Church Fathers, Universities, Monasticism, Moral Theology, Reform Movements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of failed reformers mentioned on page 12. <page-num>13</page-num>
<header>on the Secret Sciences.</header>

[...] many <note>The French text starts mid-word with "coup," likely "beaucoup" (many).</note> found it unchristian; and all good people found that this invention <note>This "invention" refers to the use of satire and mockery to expose religious corruption, mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> harmed morals more than it could benefit them, since at the very least it made known to the common people just how far the **Doctors** <note>The "Doctors" are the authoritative theologians of the Church. The author argues that satire backfired by revealing the "loopholes" or lax standards these theologians permitted.</note>, who were held in greater veneration than this Author, allowed them to relax their standards. If all these gentlemen had been willing to believe me, we would have done better than all that; but everyone is full of their own opinions, and that is how God’s business is very often delayed. It was necessary to begin by discrediting **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle's logic and philosophy were the foundation of "Scholastic" theology in the Catholic Church; to challenge Aristotle was to challenge the very structure of religious education.</note>, without making it appear that one had the intention of establishing an opposing **Philosophy**; thus, without anyone noticing, **Theology** and **Morality** would have necessarily changed their face. This would have been easy in those days; I do not know if it will be so now. Some disciples of those great men I was telling you about have taken it upon themselves to undertake it, and they are promoting, as best they can, a new—

<meta>catchword: velle (the first part of the word "nouvelle," meaning "new")</meta>

<summary>The author argues that previous attempts at reform failed because they were too direct or scandalous; he suggests that the only effective way to reform the Church is to quietly undermine Aristotelian philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Church Doctors, moral reform, Scholasticism, philosophical strategy, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of modern philosophers who have adopted his "plan" for reform but have distorted it. On the previous page, the speaker argued that to change the Church, one must first discredit Aristotle; here, he specifically identifies René Descartes as someone who stole ideas from his ancestor but added dangerous "daydreams" that undermine morality.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>-> New Conversations <-</header>

...new **Philosophy**. Since their intention is good, and as all this tends only toward continuing the plan of our **Reformation** <note>In this context, "Reformation" refers to a general intellectual and philosophical overhaul of the Church's foundations rather than the Protestant Reformation specifically.</note>, I would be grateful to them if they did not do two things. The first is to attribute to **Descartes** the glory of an invention that belongs to my **Great-great-grandfather** <note>The speaker uses the archaic French term "Trifayeul."</note> and to me. And the second is that they take as gospel <note>The original French idiom "prendre pour argent comptant" literally means "to take as ready cash," or to accept something without questioning its value.</note> all the daydreams that **Descartes** added of his own accord, which are nevertheless perfectly suited to completely ruining **Christian Morality**, if it were not already ruined.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They are quite wrong on both these points, I said to him; but I am not skilled enough to untangle what **Descartes** mixed of his own with the speculations of your Great-great-grandfather *Giordano Bruno* <note>original: *Jordanus Brunus*. Bruno (1548–1600) was an Italian friar and philosopher famous for his theories on the infinity of the universe; he was burned at the stake for heresy.</note>, whose Works I never read. "you make this supposition," I said to the **Manichaean** <note>A follower of a dualistic religion that views the world as a conflict between Light (Spirit) and Darkness (Matter).</note>, "do it anyway; then, reflecting upon this universal doubt of all things, make a demonstration of your existence, and say: 'I doubt, therefore I am.'" The Manichaean smiled. "Mr. Doctor," he said to me, "I ask you, if you please, what does 'I doubt' mean, for I have forgotten? Might it by chance be the same thing as 'I am in doubt'?" "It is that very thing," I told him. "That is to say," he continued, "that you reason learnedly and ingenuously that you are, because you are: 'I am in doubt, therefore I am' is a pleasant demonstration; and as long as you say, 'I am, therefore I am,' no one will be able to dispute that the consequence is contained within the antecedent." <note>The Manichaean is accusing the Doctor of a circular argument (tautology), suggesting that "I doubt" already assumes "I am."</note> I dismissed this mockery from the Manichaean as logical <term>chicane: the use of clever but misleading reasoning, especially in legal or philosophical arguments</term>; and hiding the slight embarrassment I was in, "You may joke as you please," I said to him, "it is certain that I think and that I know that I am, without any body having con-" <note>original: "contribué"</note>

...whose idea I possess in a form infinitely more perfect than they are: it is certain that they could not have given me this idea, for the cause must be as perfect as or more perfect than the effect. The **Manichaean** <note>A follower of the dualistic religion founded by the prophet Mani, which posited a struggle between a good realm of light and an evil realm of darkness.</note> was pensive and somber throughout this entire discourse. ### On the Secret Sciences

...as no thing in the world is good enough to give you the idea of an infinitely good principle <note>original: "principe infiniment bon" — in this philosophical context, a 'principle' refers to the primary source or origin of all things.</note>: Thus, if it were necessary for an infinitely good being to produce your idea, it would be necessary for an infinitely wicked being <note>original: "être infiniment méchant"</note> to produce mine; but both of these proofs have two great flaws. First, they suppose that it is not the nature of the understanding <note>original: "l’entendement" — the mental faculty used for reasoning and forming concepts.</note> to gather a multitude of objects into a single idea. Yet, it requires nothing else than to arrange all that it knows under certain general and universal ideas <note>The skeptic is arguing for nominalism or abstraction: that "universal" concepts are just mental shortcuts we create by grouping similar things together.</note>, and to reduce so many different beings to a certain unity. # New Dialogues

...reasoning that I would use to prove a principle of evil, you likewise cannot use it to prove your principle of good <note>original: "principe du bien" — in 17th-century philosophy, a "principle" refers to the fundamental source or origin from which everything else flows.</note>.

Beyond this flaw that I have just noted, continued this Manichaean <note>A follower of Manichaeism, a dualistic philosophy that believes the universe is a struggle between two equal powers: one Good (God) and one Evil.</note>, your demonstration has a second one which is beyond reply; it is that it assumes one can have the idea of a finite and limited thing sooner than the idea of a thing that is neither finite nor limited, and that one can know the finite sooner than the infinite. Yet to say that a line is finite is to say that it is not infinitely extended; just as saying that it is infinitely extended is to say that it is not finite. From this come those axioms <note>Self-evident truths or established principles.</note> so common and so reasonable, that the science of opposites is the same, and that relative things cannot be known without one another; this is why the idea of the infinite is as natural and as proportionate to our understanding <term>understanding: l'entendement — the mental faculty used for reasoning and forming abstract concepts.</term> as the idea of that which is finite.

As Descartes, he replied to me, did not have in view the general reformation of morals, and only wished to show off the strength of his own mind, he did not disdain to walk in the footsteps of Aristotle, whom he so greatly despised. Believing that he could at once strengthen and disguise an old and weak demonstration with a new twist, he sought to distinguish himself and wanted to capture the admiration of his readers through the boldness of his principles and his method. 

But God, who always flees from the proud who seek Him, has confounded this man, and has permitted his alleged demonstrations to repel more people from his *Physics* <term>Physics: At this time, "Physics" referred to "Natural Philosophy"—the study of the material world and the laws of nature—rather than the specific modern discipline.</term> than they have attracted. # On the Secret Sciences

...others, I will content myself with enchanting them with an infinite number of rare, singular, unheard-of, astonishing, unimaginable, and yet evident things, with which our <term>Physics<note>During this period, "Physics" (Physique) referred to Natural Philosophy—the broad study of nature and the physical world, often including what we would now call chemistry, biology, and the occult sciences.</note></term> is filled. "Gladly," he said to me; "but, Sir, you must know that <note>original: "l'oraison" — here meaning formal speaking or long-winded oratory</note> talking undermines the body a bit, and that long philosophical discourses weaken the stomach a little. "Ah! it is true," I cried out, "Mr. Jean le Brun, let us go then."

### FOURTH CONVERSATION.

A short while later, Monsieur Jean le Brun <note>A character represented as a mystic or an "Illuminist," someone who believes in direct divine inspiration.</note> woke up. "God be praised, my son," he said to me as he entered my room. "God be blessed, who watches over the salvation of His servants while they sleep, and who comes to illuminate the mists of sleep with the lights of His grace."

"Does God also speak to you while you sleep?" I asked him.

"Sometimes," he replied; "but today He did not speak to me in person; He only sent an Angel of Peace to announce His will to me, and to command me to reconcile myself with M. Descartes." <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the philosopher whose "mechanical" view of the world—viewing nature like a clockwork machine—was often seen as a threat to traditional spiritual views, yet here is being strangely embraced by a mystic.</note>

"Undoubtedly," I answered him.

"Then you do not know," he added, "that <term>extension<note>In Cartesian philosophy—the system of René Descartes—'extension' (étenduë) is the primary attribute of physical matter. It means that matter is defined solely by the fact that it occupies space (length, width, and depth).</note></term>—that is to say, length, width, and depth—is the <term>essence<note>The fundamental nature or defining quality of a thing.</note></term> of matter?"

"Even if that were so," I resumed, "would it follow that God did not create it?"

"Yes," he retorted, "because it would follow that it is impossible to imagine a moment when this matter did not exist; and here is the little argument I make, to which there is certainly no answer. One must necessarily say that a thing exists when one cannot in any way conceive of it not existing: Now, the fact is that one cannot in any way conceive of matter not existing."

Supposing that God is the sole Author of all action, all that can be said to explain the union of the soul to the body is that God has established a certain relationship between the body and the soul. He has made a **pact** <note>This "pact" refers to the theory of Occasionalism, popularized by Nicolas Malebranche, which suggests that created things do not have their own causal power; instead, God acts on the "occasion" of a physical or mental event.</note> that every time a certain movement occurs in the body, He will produce a corresponding thought in the soul; and that every time the soul thinks in a certain way, a corresponding movement will be produced in the body.

[...ordinary course] <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "God, having obliged himself, in the ordinary course..."</note> of Nature, to always produce the **rational soul** <note>The "rational soul" refers to the human mind or spirit, distinguished from the "sensitive" or "vegetative" souls attributed to animals and plants in classical philosophy.</note> every time the **embryo** shall be formed, and that the matter shall be in such and such a disposition, it is clear that matter thus disposed would be the **physical cause** of the rational soul; and since a Spirit cannot be the effect of a body, one would have to look elsewhere than in spirituality for the essence of the soul and the reason for its immortality.

"But in what," I said to him, "do you make the difference consist between the **physical cause** and the **moral cause**?"

"I know of no other," he replied, "and I seek none, because I wish that none other could be found than this one. **Faith** would be no better for it; for besides the aforementioned difficulties, it would still have enough others to overcome. For example, my reason could tell me whenever it pleased that my soul is physically united with the **Holy Spirit**; for am I not free to explain **Grace** by a completely similar union,"

### FIFTH CONVERSATION.

"Good evening, Mr. Jean le Brun," I said, returning to my room after having finished writing, "did you sup well and without distraction?"

"Very well, by the grace of God," he replied to me. "I meditated during the entire meal on the extravagance of certain **heretics** <note>original: "Hérétiques"</note> I saw in **Germany**, called **Ubiquitists** <term>Ubiquitists: a term used here to describe certain Lutheran theologians who argued that because Christ is God, and God is everywhere, Christ's physical body must also be omnipresent (ubiquitous). This had major implications for how they understood the Eucharist.</term>, who believe they receive communion every time they eat because they imagine that the Body of **Jesus Christ** is everywhere. Must one not be insane to say such a thing? For if **extension** and **impenetrability** <note>These are the two defining characteristics of matter in Cartesian philosophy. "Extension" means taking up space in three dimensions, and "impenetrability" means that two pieces of matter cannot occupy the same space at the same time.</note> constitute the essence of **matter**, is it not just as impossible for one body to be received within another body of equal or lesser extension, as it is impossible for a **cubic body** of nine feet to be enclosed within the space of a cubic body of three feet? What is most ridiculous about these Ubiquitists is that they believe

"Wait," I told him, "you will certainly be satisfied with him. There are things, according to this **Holy Doctor** <note>A title for Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), also known as the "Angelic Doctor," whose work remains the foundation of Catholic theology</note>, which are essentially impossible, and there are those which are impossible only by accident. 

A thing is essentially impossible when it cannot happen without implying a **contradiction** <note>A logical impossibility where a thing would have to be two opposite things at once, such as a "square circle"</note>, and without one being able to say of it, or of some other thing, that *this is* and *this is not* all at once. 

A thing is impossible by accident when, in truth, it does not imply a contradiction for it to happen, but it cannot occur in the ordinary course of nature—even though it could happen through an extraordinary arrangement by God. 

The first impossibility is usually attached to the **essences** <term>Essence</term> <note>The fundamental nature of a thing; the qualities that make it what it is and without which it would cease to exist</note> of things, and the second to their properties and **accidents** <term>Accident</term> <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a characteristic that a thing has (like color or weight) but which is not essential to its basic nature</note>. An **Angel**, for example, cannot eat and drink, because the nature of spirit is only to think and to will; and there would be a con-

As one must take people by their weak point, I would have shown him that he was thereby giving a great advantage to <note>Valentinus was a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher who proposed that the divine world consisted of numerous emanations</note> Valentinus, his adversary, and father of the Valentinians, just as Praxeas was of the Praxeans; for, I would have said to him, if God can change the essence of things, he can make it so that two and one make thirty, and not three: so that it is not impossible for the Divinity to be multiplied into thirty <term>Aeons<note>In Gnostic theology, Aeons are various levels of beings or powers that emanate from the supreme, unknowable God</note></term>, as Valentinus imagines, and that from these thirty Aeons there results yet another swarm of Divinities, for which <note>Tertullian was an early Christian author known for his polemics against heresy; he wrote a specific treatise titled "Against the Valentinians"</note> Tertullian mocks Valentinus for having had the generosity to enrich the Heavens; by this means, I would have surely brought Praxeas back to my opinion.

<summary>Jean le Brun argues that if God could arbitrarily change the essence of things or the past, it would validate Gnostic heresies like those of Valentinus, who claimed the divine was divided into thirty Aeons. He concludes that logical consistency is necessary to prevent theological absurdity.</summary>

"It would be desirable, Monsieur Jean le Brun," I said, "that you should also lead back all those who make a profession of explaining or following <term>Descartes<note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a French philosopher whose rationalist approach sought to explain the world through reason and mechanics, often creating tension with traditional Church teachings.</note></term>; for they all say in common agreement that they do not wish to set any limits to the **omnipotence of God**, and under the guise of respect and submission, they allow for a hundred contradictory suppositions."

"Do not trouble yourself with that," replied Jean le Brun, "and remember the political reasons one may have for speaking in such a way; provided that the truths of the **Faith** are well contested by this **Philosophy**, **Morality** will fare well, and let us not worry about the rest. I praise God that, above all, it combats the mystery of the **Eucharist** <note>The Eucharist, or Holy Communion, was a major point of philosophical contention for Cartesians; Descartes's definition of matter made the Catholic doctrine of Transubstantiation—where the substance of bread changes but its physical "accidents" remain—difficult to explain logically.</note> in so many respects that it is impossible for this mystery to ever be reconciled with any of our Principles."

...that what makes animals live and move is nothing but a certain arrangement of the parts of matter; just as what makes a clock go is nothing but a certain arrangement of its wheels. <meta>This page continues a dialogue regarding the Cartesian theory of "animal-machines." The speaker is questioning whether the mechanical laws of physics proposed by René Descartes can truly account for the seemingly intelligent behavior of animals, using a famous anecdote about a chess-playing monkey to challenge the theory.</meta>

### on the Secret Sciences.

"Mr. Descartes founded this entire Philosophy upon that principle. It would seem then," I replied, "that he was quite sure of it. Well then, with that, will you explain to us everything that animals <note>original: 'bêtes' — in this period, the term referred to all non-human animals, often used to emphasize their supposed lack of reason.</note> do?"

"Everything," he said.

"Even that surprising action," I continued, "of the monkey <note>original: 'guenon' — specifically a female monkey or ape.</note> belonging to a King of Poland?"

"What did she do?" he asked.

"Something showing great common sense," I went on. "She played chess every day with the King."

"Chess!" cried Jean le Brun. "The game of chess is a game of reasoning; one must even have a fair amount of intellect to play it; there are a thousand people who are not capable of it."

"Yet this monkey was capable," I replied; "she played chess, and played it very well. One day, after having contested a match for a long time, she played so well that she gave checkmate. The King, stung by this, gave her a great slap."

"He was in the wrong!" cried Jean le Brun; "but is this not a moral tale <note>original: 'apologue'</note>, and one of those fashionable fables you are telling me?"

"It is a true story," [I told] him...

### on the Secret Sciences.

Jean le Brun replied; "For if we were to grant that animals <term>animals (bêtes): literally 'beasts,' used in this era to distinguish non-human creatures from humans.</term> think, and that subtle matter <term>subtle matter (matière subtile): in Cartesian physics, an invisible, fluid-like substance that fills all space and transmits motion and light.</term> in its movement could create the sensation we call thought, we would soon be troubled regarding the rational soul and what becomes of the souls of animals after death. This is why a great Englishman named More <note>original: 'Morus' — Henry More (1614–1687), a philosopher and 'Cambridge Platonist' who initially admired Descartes but later critiqued his mechanical view of the soul.</note> believed that Monsieur Descartes preferred to say that animals have no soul at all, rather than be forced to answer certain troublesome minds—of which this century has plenty—who mix religion into everything and drag Faith into every dispute. These are idle people, unworthy of practicing philosophy, who would certainly have asked: 'What becomes of this animal soul? Why is it not immortal and spiritual, since it thinks? Or why is the human soul immortal simply because it thinks?' That is why we always wisely resort to a certain general answer, which rids us of all these little anecdotes—"

<summary>Jean le Brun explains that Descartes denied animal souls primarily to avoid theological complications regarding the immortality of the soul. ...after dinner, to see if what this Surgeon said is true. O God! Could it be possible that a genius as great as Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher whose "system" of mechanical philosophy attempted to explain the world through reason and mathematics rather than traditional ancient authorities.</note> had based an entire system on things that mere surgeon’s apprentices <note>original: "Fraters de Chirurgien"—a derogatory term for lowly barber-surgeons or their assistants, implying they lack formal education.</note> could prove false? If that is so, there is no more point in saying that neither I nor my companions can ever reform Morality through this Philosophy. Alas! we must let that of Aristotle <note>Aristotle's philosophy dominated European universities for centuries (Scholasticism); the speaker views its potential "triumph" as a intellectual defeat.</note> flourish. For my part, rather than see it triumph so, I wish to die; the resolution is made.

I would advise you, Monsieur, I said to him, to reconcile with Aristotle before dying; otherwise, you will have this Man on your mind in the other world, who will distress you; and his angry shadow will always be after yours, to make a hundred annoying reproaches. So you assume that I will be damned, he replied: You remind me of a certain Father Le Brun <note>Likely a reference to Pierre Le Brun (1661–1729), a French Oratorian priest who wrote extensively against superstitions and "secret" sciences.</note> my cousin and fellow countryman, who was always telling me that, who took me in...

"Did Aristotle say all those things?" interrupted Jean le Brun. "Yes," I told him. "But," he replied, "that is precisely our own Philosophy. I was therefore quite wrong not to have read Aristotle in my youth. Descartes is the cause of it; he had read him thoroughly. I found him one day working on the *Third Book of the Soul* <note>Referring to Aristotle’s *De Anima*, a foundational text on psychology and perception.</note>: he told me that Aristotle was of his own opinion regarding the manner in which sensation occurs. He said he was delighted that this Philosopher had, for once in his life, known the truth, and had perceived that all sensations are produced by touch. <note>The "mechanical philosophy" of the 17th century argued that senses like sight and smell were actually just particles or waves physically "touching" our organs, just like the sense of touch.</note> Since I saw that there was only this one good passage in Aristotle, I resolved not to waste any time reading him."

To tell you the truth, the slight irritation I felt toward you yesterday came from the fact that it seemed to me a matter of bad faith to rail as you did against Aristotle, and to hurl a thousand curses against his <term>Enthymemes<note>A type of logical argument where one of the premises is implied rather than stated.</note></term> and his <term>Syllogisms<note>A formal style of logic where a conclusion is reached via two premises (e.g., all men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal).</note></term>; yet I see clearly that you have never read him.

It is true, he replied to me; but Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), whose "New Philosophy" sought to replace the traditional Aristotelian methods taught in schools.</note> had spoken so much ill of him to me; and furthermore, this certain Father le Brun <note>Likely a reference to Pierre Le Brun (1661–1729), a priest who wrote extensively on the history of religious ceremonies and superstitions.</note> of whom I spoke to you, has so unsettled me with his Aristotle—he cited him so often in the disputes we had together, and drummed it into my ears so much—that he has given me a mortal aversion to him. It has reached the point that as soon as I hear the name of Aristotle, I feel as if I see Father le Brun at my heels, chasing me out of Ireland and making me pass for a Calvinist <note>A follower of the Protestant theological system of John Calvin; in this context, used as an accusation of heresy.</note>.
